Appendix 30.G
Homework problems

AD.1
Independent but not IID data

Assume that the
Y_{i}
’s are independent with mean
\mu_{i}
and variances
\sigma_{i}^{2}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
. The parameter of interest is

\mu=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\mu_{i}.

Show that

\hat{\mu}=n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Y_{i}

is unbiased for
\mu
and find its variance. Show that the usual variance estimator for IID data

\hat{v}=\{n(n-1)\}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-\hat{\mu})^{2}

is a conservative estimator for the variance of
\hat{\mu}
in the sense that

E(\hat{v})-\textup{var}(\hat{\mu})=\{n(n-1)\}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\mu_{i}-\mu)^{2}\geq 0.

Remark: Consider a simpler case with
\mu_{i}=\mu
and
\sigma_{i}^{2}=\sigma^{2}
for all
i=1,\ldots,n
. The sample mean is unbiased for
\mu
with variance
\sigma^{2}/n
. Moreover, an unbiased estimator for the variance
\sigma^{2}/n
is
\hat{\sigma}^{2}/n=\hat{v}
, where
\hat{\sigma}^{2}=(n-1)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(Y_{i}-\hat{\mu})^{2}
.
This problem states a more general result for the case with independent but not identically distributed observations. The result has an important implication for Theorem
7.1
for the matched-pairs experiment discussed in Chapter
7
.

AD.2
Asymptotic Normality of ratio

Prove (
155
).

AD.3
Asymptotic Normality of product

Prove (
157
).

AD.4
Product of two independent Normals

Assume
X\sim\textsc{N}(\mu_{X},\sigma_{X}^{2}),Y\sim\textsc{N}(\mu_{Y},\sigma_{Y}^{2})
and
X\begin{picture}\put(0.0,0.0){\line{1}{0}{9.0}}\put(3.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\put(6.0,0.0){\line{0}{1}{8.0}}\end{picture}Y
. Show that

\textup{var}(XY)=\sigma_{X}^{2}\sigma_{Y}^{2}+\mu_{X}^{2}\sigma_{Y}^{2}+\mu_{Y}^{2}\sigma_{X}^{2}.

Remark: This problem gives a non-asymptotic form of Example
30.2
.

AD.5
Variance estimators in two-by-two tables

Use the delta method to show the variance estimators in Section
30.C.2
.

AD.6
Fisher weighting

Assume that we have
p
independent unbiased estimators
\hat{\theta}_{1},\ldots,\hat{\theta}_{p}
for a common parameter
\theta
:

E(\hat{\theta}_{j})=\theta,\quad(j=1,\ldots,p).

The estimator
\hat{\theta}_{j}
has variance
v_{j}

(j=1,\ldots,p)
.

Construct a new estimator

\hat{\theta}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}w_{j}\hat{\theta}_{j}

with nonrandom constants
w_{j}
’s.
Show that
\hat{\theta}
is unbiased for estimating
\theta
if
\sum_{j=1}^{p}w_{j}=1
. Show that the optimal
w_{j}
’s such that
\hat{\theta}
has the smallest variance under the constraint
\sum_{j=1}^{p}w_{j}=1
are

w_{j}^{*}=\frac{1/v_{j}}{\sum_{j^{\prime}=1}^{p}1/v_{j^{\prime}}},\quad(j=1,\ldots,p).

Show that the resulting estimator
\hat{\theta}^{*}=\sum_{j=1}^{p}w_{j}^{*}\hat{\theta}_{j}
has variance

\textup{var}(\hat{\theta}^{*})=\frac{1}{\sum_{j=1}^{p}1/v_{j}}.

Remark: This is called Fisher weighting. The optimal weights are proportional to the inverses of the variances.

Chapter 31
Linear and Logistic Regressions

Appendix 31.A
Population ordinary least squares

Assume that
(x_{i},y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{IID}}}{{\sim}}(x,y)
, where
x
is a
p
-dimensional random scalar or vector and
y
is a random scalar.
Below I will use
(x,y)
to denote a general observation, dropping the subscript
i
for simplicity.
Define the population ordinary least squares (OLS) coefficient as

\beta=\arg\min_{b}E\left\{(y-x^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}b)^{2}\right\}.

The objective function is quadratic in
b
, so we can show that the minimizer is

\beta=\left\{E\left(xx^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\right)\right\}^{-1}E\left(xy\right)

if the moments exist and
E(xx^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}})
is invertible.

With
\beta
, we can define
x^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\beta
as the
linear projection
of
y
on
x
, and define

\varepsilon=y-x^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\beta

(158)

as the
population residual
.
By the definition of
\beta
, we can verify that

E(x\varepsilon)=E\left\{x(y-x^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\beta)\right\}=E(xy)-E(xx^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}})\beta=0.

Example 31.1
(population OLS with the intercept)

If we include
1
as a component of
x
, then

E(\varepsilon)=E(y-x^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\beta)=0

which further implies that
\textup{cov}(x,\varepsilon)=0
.
So with an intercept in
\beta
, the mean of the population residual
must be zero, and it is uncorrelated with other covariates by construction.

Example 31.2
(univariate population OLS with the intercept)

An important special case is that for scalars
x
and
y
, we can define

(\alpha,\beta)=\arg\min_{a,b}E\{(y-a-bx)^{2}\}

which have explicit formulas

\beta=\frac{\textup{cov}(x,y)}{\textup{var}(x)},\quad\alpha=E(y)-\beta E(x).

Example 31.3
(univariate population OLS without an intercept)

Without intercept, we can define

\gamma=\arg\min_{c}E\{(y-cx)^{2}\}

which equals

\gamma=\frac{E(xy)}{E(x^{2})}.

When
x
has mean zero,
\gamma
equals the
\beta
in Example
31.2
.

We can also rewrite (
158
) as

y=x^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\beta+\varepsilon,

(159)

which holds by the definition of the population OLS coefficient and residual without any modeling
assumption. We call (
159
) the population
OLS decomposition.

Appendix 31.B
Sample ordinary least squares

Based on data
(x_{i},y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{IID}}}{{\sim}}(x,y)
, we can easily
obtain the moment estimator for the population OLS coefficient

\hat{\beta}=\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\right)^{-1}\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}y_{i}\right),

and the residuals
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}=y_{i}-x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\hat{\beta}.

This is called the sample OLS or simply the OLS. The OLS coefficient

\hat{\beta}
minimizes the residual sum of squares

\hat{\beta}=\arg\min_{b}n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(y_{i}-x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}b)^{2},

so it must satisfy

\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}(y_{i}-x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\hat{\beta})=0,

which is sometimes called the
Normal equation.

The fitted values, also called the
linear projections
of
y_{i}
on
x_{i}
, equal

\hat{y}_{i}=x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\hat{\beta}\quad(i=1,\ldots,n).

Using the matrix notation

X=\begin{pmatrix}x_{1}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\\
\vdots\\
x_{n}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\end{pmatrix},\quad Y=\begin{pmatrix}y_{1}\\
\vdots\\
y_{n}\end{pmatrix},

we can write the OLS coefficient as

\hat{\beta}=(X^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}X)^{-1}X^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}Y

and the fitted vector as

\hat{Y}=X\hat{\beta}=X(X^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}X)^{-1}X^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}Y.

Define the hat matrix as

H=X(X^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}X)^{-1}X^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}.

Then we also have
\hat{Y}=HY
, justifying the name “hat matrix.” The diagonal elements of
H
,
h_{ii}
’s, are often called the
leverage scores
.

Assuming finite fourth moments of
(x,y)
, we can use the law of large numbers and the CLT to show that

\sqrt{n}(\hat{\beta}-\beta)\rightarrow\text{N}(0,V)

in distribution with
V=B^{-1}MB^{-1}
, where
B=E(xx^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}})
and
M=E(\varepsilon^{2}xx^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}})
.
So a moment estimator for the asymptotic variance of
\hat{\beta}
is

\hat{V}_{\textsc{ehw}}=n^{-1}\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\right)^{-1}\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}x_{i}x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\right)\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\right)^{-1},

(160)

which is called the Eicker–Huber–White (EHW) robust covariance
estimator
(
Eicker, 1967
;
Huber, 1967
;
White, 1980
)
. We can show that
n\hat{V}_{\textsc{ehw}}\rightarrow V
in probability. Based on
\hat{\beta}
and
\hat{V}_{\textsc{ehw}}
, we can make inference about the population OLS coefficient
\beta
.

There are many variants of the EHW robust covariance estimator based on the leverage scores
(
Long and Ervin, 2000
)
. In particular, the HC1 variant modifies
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}
to
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}/(n-p)
,
the HC2 variant modifies
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}
to
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}/(1-h_{ii})
,
and
the HC3 variant modifies
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}
to
\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}/(1-h_{ii})^{2}
, in the definition of
\hat{V}_{\textsc{ehw}}
.

Appendix 31.C
Frisch–Waugh–Lovell Theorem

The Frisch–Waugh–Lovell (FWL) theorem has two versions: one at the population level and the other at the sample level. It reduces multivariate OLS to univariate OLS and therefore facilitates the understanding and calculation of the OLS coefficients. Below I will present special cases of the FWL theorem which are enough for this book.

Theorem 31.1
(population FWL)

The coefficient of
x_{1}
in the OLS fit of
y
on
(x_{1},x_{2},\ldots,x_{p})
equals the coefficient of
\tilde{x}_{1}
in the OLS fit of
y
or
\tilde{y}
on
\tilde{x}_{1}
, where
\tilde{y}
is the residual from the OLS fit of
y
on
(x_{2},\ldots,x_{p})
and
\tilde{x}_{1}
is the residual from the OLS fit of
x_{1}
on
(x_{2},\ldots,x_{p})
.

In Theorem
31.1
, residualizing
x_{1}
is crucial but residualizing
y
is not.

Theorem 31.2
(sample FWL)

With data
(Y,X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{p})
containing column vectors, the coefficient of
X_{1}
in the OLS fit of
Y
on
(X_{1},X_{2},\ldots,X_{p})
equals the coefficient of
\tilde{X}_{1}
in the OLS fit of
Y
or
\tilde{Y}
on
\tilde{X}_{1}
, where
\tilde{Y}
is the residual vector from the OLS fit of
Y
on
(X_{2},\ldots,X_{p})
and
\tilde{X}_{1}
is the residual vector from the OLS fit of
X_{1}
on
(X_{2},\ldots,X_{p})
.

Again, in Theorem
31.2
, residualizing
X_{1}
is crucial but residualizing
Y
is not.

Ding, 2021
gives more numerical properties related to the sample FWL theorem.

Appendix 31.D
Linear model

Sometimes, we impose a stronger model assumption which requires the conditional mean of
y
given
x
is linear:

E(y\mid x)=x^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\beta

or, equivalently,

y=x^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\beta+\varepsilon\qquad\text{with}\qquad E(\varepsilon\mid x)=0,

which is called the restricted mean model. Under this model, the population OLS coefficient is the true parameter of interest:

\displaystyle\left\{E(xx^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}})\right\}^{-1}E(xy)

\displaystyle=\left\{E(xx^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}})\right\}^{-1}E\left\{xE(y\mid x)\right\}

\displaystyle=\left\{E(xx^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}})\right\}^{-1}E(xx^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\beta)

\displaystyle=\beta.

Moreover, the population OLS coefficient does not depend on the distribution
of
x
. The asymptotic inference in Section
31.A
applies to this model too.

In the special case with
\textup{var}(\varepsilon\mid x)=\sigma^{2}
,
the asymptotic variance of the OLS coefficient reduces to

V=\sigma^{2}\{E(xx^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}})\}^{-1}

so a simpler moment estimator for the asymptotic variance of
\hat{\beta}
is

\hat{V}_{\textsc{ols}}=\hat{\sigma}^{2}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\right)^{-1}

(161)

where
\hat{\sigma}^{2}=(n-p)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\hat{\varepsilon}_{i}^{2}
is an unbiased estimator for
\sigma^{2}
, recalling that
n
denotes the sample size and
p
denotes the dimension of
x
. This is the standard covariance estimator from the
lm
function.

Based on the
BostonHousing
data, we first obtain the standard output from the
lm
function.

Appendix 31.E
Weighted least squares

Assuming that
(w_{i},x_{i},y_{i})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{IID}}}{{\sim}}(w,x,y)
with
w\neq 0
. At the population level, we can define the weighted least squares (WLS) coefficient as

\beta_{w}=\arg\min_{b}E\{w(y-x^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}b)^{2}\},

which satisfies

E\{wx(y-x^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\beta_{w})\}=0

and thus equals

\beta_{w}=\{E(wxx^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}})\}^{-1}E(wxy)

if
E(wxx^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}})
is invertible.

At the sample level, we can define the WLS coefficient as

\hat{\beta}_{w}=\arg\min_{b}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}(y_{i}-x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}b)^{2},

which satisfies

\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}x_{i}(y_{i}-x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\hat{\beta}_{w})=0

and thus equals

\hat{\beta}_{w}=\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}x_{i}x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\right)^{-1}\left(n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}x_{i}y_{i}\right)

if
\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}x_{i}x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}
is invertible.

In
R
, we can specify
weights
in the
lm
function to implement WLS.

Appendix 31.F
Logistic regression

31.F.1
Model

Technically, we can apply the OLS procedure even if the outcome
y
is binary. However, it is a little awkward to have predicted probabilities outside the range of
[0,1]
. This motivates us to consider the
following model:

\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i})=g(x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\beta),

where
g(\cdot):\mathbb{R}\rightarrow[0,1]
is a monotone function,
and its inverse is often called the
link function
. The
g(\cdot)
function can be any distribution function of a random variable, but we will focus on the logistic form:

g(z)=\frac{e^{z}}{1+e^{z}}=(1+e^{-z})^{-1}.

We can also write the logistic model
as

\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i})=\frac{e^{x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\beta}}{1+e^{x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\beta}},

or, equivalently, with the definition
\text{logit}(z)=\log\frac{z}{1-z}
, we have

\text{logit}\left\{\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i})\right\}=x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\beta.

Assume that
x_{i1}
is binary.
Under the logistic model, we have

\displaystyle\beta_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\text{logit}\left\{\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i1}=1,\ldots)\right\}-\text{logit}\left\{\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i1}=0,\ldots)\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\log\frac{\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i1}=1,\ldots)/\textup{pr}(y_{i}=0\mid x_{i1}=1,\ldots)}{\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i1}=0,\ldots)/\textup{pr}(y_{i}=0\mid x_{i1}=0,\ldots)},

where
\ldots
contains all other regressor
x_{i2},\ldots,x_{ip}
.
Therefore, the coefficient
\beta_{1}
equals the log of the odds ratio of
x_{i1}
on
y_{i}
conditional on other regressors.

31.F.2
Maximum likelihood estimate

Let
\textup{pr}(y_{i}=1\mid x_{i})=\pi(x_{i},\beta)
.
To estimate the parameter
\beta
, we can maximize the following likelihood function:

\displaystyle L(\beta)

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\pi(x_{i},\beta)\right\}^{y_{i}}\left\{1-\pi(x_{i},\beta)\right\}^{1-y_{i}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left\{\frac{\pi(x_{i},\beta)}{1-\pi(x_{i},\beta)}\right\}^{y_{i}}\left\{1-\pi(x_{i},\beta)\right\}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\left(e^{x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\beta}\right)^{y_{i}}\frac{1}{1+e^{x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\beta}}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=1}^{n}\frac{e^{y_{i}x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\beta}}{1+e^{x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\beta}}.

Let
\hat{\beta}
denote the maximizer, which is called the maximum likelihood estimate (MLE). Taking the log of
L(\beta)
and differentiating it with respect to
\beta
, we can show that the MLE must satisfy the first-order condition:

\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}\{y_{i}-\pi(x_{i},\hat{\beta})\}=0.

If
x_{i}
contains the intercept, the MLE must satisfy

\sum_{i=1}^{n}\{y_{i}-\pi(x_{i},\hat{\beta})\}=0,

that is, the average of the observed
y_{i}
’s must be identical to the average of the fitted probabilities
\pi(x_{i},\hat{\beta})
’s.

Using the general theory for the MLE, we can show that it is consistent for the true parameter
\beta
and is asymptotically Normal:

\sqrt{n}(\hat{\beta}-\beta)\rightarrow\text{N}(0,V)

in distribution, where

V=E[\pi(x_{i},\beta)\{1-\pi(x_{i},\beta)\}xx^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}].

So we can approximate the covariance matrix of
\hat{\beta}
by

n^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\pi(x_{i},\hat{\beta})\{1-\pi(x_{i},\hat{\beta})\}x_{i}x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}.

In
R
, the
glm
function can find the MLE and report the estimated covariance matrix. We use the
lalonde
data to illustrate the logistic regression with the binary outcome indicating positive real earnings in 1978.

31.F.3
Extension to the case-control study

In case-control studies, sampling is conditional on the binary outcome, that is, units with outcomes
y_{i}=1
and
y_{i}=0
are sampled with different probabilities. Let
s_{i}
be the sampling indicator. In case-control studies, we have

\textup{pr}(s_{i}=1\mid x_{i},y_{i})=\textup{pr}(s_{i}=1\mid y_{i})

as a function of
y_{i}
, and we only observe units with
s_{i}=1
.

Based on the model and the sampling mechanism of the case-control study, it does not seem obvious whether or not we can still use logistic regression to estimate the coefficients. Nevertheless,
Prentice and Pyke, 1979
proved a positive results. In case-control studies, logistic regression can still consistently estimate all the coefficients except for the intercept.

31.F.4
Logistic regression with weights

Sometimes, unit
i
has weight
w_{i}
. Then we can fit a weighted logistic regression by solving

\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}x_{i}\{y_{i}-\pi(x_{i},\hat{\beta})\}=0.

In
R
, we can specify
weights
in the
glm
function to implement weighted logistic regression.

Appendix 31.G
Homework problems

AE.1
Sample WLS with the intercept

Assume the regressor
x_{i}
contains the intercept. Show that

\bar{y}_{w}=\bar{x}_{w}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\hat{\beta}_{w}

(162)

where
\bar{x}_{w}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}x_{i}/\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}
and
\bar{y}_{w}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}y_{i}/\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}
are the weighted averages of the
x_{i}
’s and
y_{i}
’s.

AE.2
Population OLS with a binary regressor

Assume
x
is binary. Define the population OLS:

(\alpha,\beta)=\arg\min_{(a,b)}E\{(y-a-bx)^{2}\}.

Show that
\beta=E(y\mid x=1)-E(y\mid x=0)
and
\alpha=E(y\mid x=0)
.

AE.3
Univariate WLS

As a special case of WLS, define

(\hat{\alpha}_{w},\hat{\beta}_{w})=\arg\min_{(a,b)}\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}(y_{i}-a-bx_{i})^{2}

where
w_{i}\geq 0
. Show that

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{w}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}(x_{i}-\bar{x}_{w})(y_{i}-\bar{y}_{w})}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}(x_{i}-\bar{x}_{w})^{2}}

(163)

and

\displaystyle\hat{\alpha}_{w}=\bar{y}_{w}-\hat{\beta}_{w}\bar{x}_{w},

(164)

where
\bar{x}_{w}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}x_{i}/\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}
and
\bar{y}_{w}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}y_{i}/\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}
are the weighted averages of the
x_{i}
’s and
y_{i}
’s.

Further assume that the
x_{i}
’s are binary. Show that

\displaystyle\hat{\beta}_{w}=\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}x_{i}y_{i}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}x_{i}}-\frac{\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}(1-x_{i})y_{i}}{\sum_{i=1}^{n}w_{i}(1-x_{i})}.

(165)

That is, if the regressor is binary in the univariate WLS, the coefficient of the regressor equals the difference in the weighted means.

Remark: To prove (
165
), use an appropriate reparametrization of the WLS problem. Otherwise, the derivation can be tedious.

AE.4
OLS with orthogonal regressors

Consider sample OLS fit of an
n
-vector
Y
on an
n\times p
matrix
X
, with coefficient
\hat{\beta}
. Partition
X
into
X=(X_{1},X_{2})
, where
X_{1}
is an
n\times k
matrix and
X_{2}
is an
n\times l
matrix, with
p=k+l
. Correspondingly, partition
\hat{\beta}
into

\hat{\beta}=\begin{pmatrix}\hat{\beta}_{1}\\
\hat{\beta}_{2}\end{pmatrix}.

Assume
X_{1}
and
X_{2}
are orthogonal, that is,
X_{1}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}X_{2}=0
. Show that
\hat{\beta}_{1}
equals the coefficient from OLS of
Y
on
X_{1}
and
\hat{\beta}_{2}
equals the coefficient from OLS of
Y
on
X_{2}
, respectively.

AE.5
OLS with a non-degenerate transformation of the regressors

Define
\hat{\beta}
as the coefficient from the sample OLS fit of an
n
-vector
Y
on an
n\times p
matrix
X
.
Let
\Gamma
be a
p\times p
non-degenerate matrix, and define
X^{\prime}=X\Gamma
.
Define
\hat{\beta}^{\prime}
as the coefficient from the sample OLS fit of
Y
on
X^{\prime}
.

Show that

\hat{\beta}=\Gamma\hat{\beta}^{\prime}.

AE.6
Variances of the OLS estimator

Assume
(x_{i},y_{i})_{i=1}^{n}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\textup{IID}}}{{\sim}}(x,y)
and

E(y\mid x)=x^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\beta,\quad\textup{var}(y\mid x)=\sigma^{2}(x).

Show that the OLS estimator
\hat{\beta}
has conditional mean

E(\hat{\beta}\mid x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\beta

and conditional variance

\textup{var}(\hat{\beta}\mid x_{1},\ldots,x_{n})=\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\right)^{-1}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}\sigma^{2}(x_{i})x_{i}x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\right)\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}x_{i}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}\right)^{-1}.

Remark: This problem may provide some intuition for the variance estimators
(
160
) and (
161
).

Chapter 32
Some Useful Lemmas for Simple Random Sampling From a Finite Population

\chaptermark

Simple Random Sampling

Appendix 32.A
Lemmas

Simple random sampling is a basic topic in standard survey sampling textbooks
(
Cochran, 1953
, e.g.,)
. Below I review some results for simple random sampling that are useful for design-based inference in the CRE in Chapters
3
and
4
. I define simple random sampling based on the distribution of the inclusion indicators.

Definition 32.1
(simple random sampling)

A simple random sample of size
n_{1}
consists of a subset from a finite population of
n
units indexed by
i=1,\ldots,n
.
Let
\bm{Z}=(Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{n})
be the inclusion indicators of the
n
units with
Z_{i}=1
if unit
i
is sampled and
Z_{i}=0
otherwise.
The vector
\bm{Z}
can take
\binom{n}{n_{1}}
possible permutations of a vector of
n_{1}
1’s and
n_{0}
0’s, and each has equal probability.

By Definition
32.1
, simple random sampling is a special form of
sampling without replacement
because it does not allow for repeatedly sampling the same unit. Lemma
32.1
below summarizes the first two moments of the inclusion indicators.

Lemma 32.1

Under simple random sampling, we have

E(Z_{i})=\frac{n_{1}}{n},\quad\textup{var}(Z_{i})=\frac{n_{1}n_{0}}{n^{2}},\quad\textup{cov}(Z_{i},Z_{j})=-\frac{n_{1}n_{0}}{n^{2}(n-1)}.

In more compact forms, we have

E(\bm{Z})=\frac{n_{1}}{n}\bm{1}_{n},\quad\textup{cov}(\bm{Z})=\frac{n_{1}n_{0}}{n(n-1)}\bm{P}_{n},

where
\bm{1}_{n}
is a
n
-dimensional vector of
1
’s, and
\bm{P}_{n}=\bm{I}_{n}-n^{-1}\bm{1}_{n}\bm{1}_{n}^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}
is the
n\times n
projection matrix orthogonal to
\bm{1}_{n}.

Let
\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\}
be a finite population with mean
\bar{c}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}c_{i}/n
and variance

S_{c}^{2}=(n-1)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}-\bar{c})^{2};

let
\{d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}\}
be another finite population with mean
\bar{d}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}d_{i}/n
and variance

S_{d}^{2}=(n-1)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(d_{i}-\bar{d})^{2};

their covariance is

S_{cd}=(n-1)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}-\bar{c})(d_{i}-\bar{d}).

Under simple random sampling, the sample means are

\hat{\bar{c}}=n_{1}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}c_{i},\quad\hat{\bar{d}}=n_{1}^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}d_{i};

sample variances are

\hat{S}_{c}^{2}=(n_{1}-1)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}(c_{i}-\hat{\bar{c}})^{2},\quad\hat{S}_{d}^{2}=(n_{1}-1)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}(d_{i}-\hat{\bar{d}})^{2};

the sample covariance is

\hat{S}_{cd}=(n_{1}-1)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}(c_{i}-\hat{\bar{c}})(d_{i}-\hat{\bar{d}}).

Lemma
32.2
below gives the moments of the sample means
\hat{\bar{c}}
and
\hat{\bar{d}}
.

Lemma 32.2

Under simple random sampling, the sample means are unbiased for the population means:

E(\hat{\bar{c}})=\bar{c},\quad E(\hat{\bar{d}})=\bar{d}.

Their variances and covariance are

\displaystyle\textup{var}\left(\hat{\bar{c}}\right)=\frac{n_{0}}{nn_{1}}S_{c}^{2},\quad\textup{var}\left(\hat{\bar{d}}\right)=\frac{n_{0}}{nn_{1}}S_{d}^{2},\quad\textup{cov}\left(\hat{\bar{c}},\hat{\bar{d}}\right)=\frac{n_{0}}{nn_{1}}S_{cd}.

In the variance formulas in Lemma
32.2
, the coefficient
n_{0}/(nn_{1})=1/n_{1}\times(1-n_{1}/n)
in Lemma
32.2
is different from
1/n_{1}
under IID sampling. The additional term
1-n_{1}/n=n_{0}/n
is called the
finite population correction
factor.

Lemma
32.3
below gives the unbiasedness of the sample variances and covariance for estimating the population analogs.

Lemma 32.3

Under simple random sampling, the sample variances and covariance are unbiased for their population versions:

E(\hat{S}_{c}^{2})=S_{c}^{2},\quad E(\hat{S}_{d}^{2})=S_{d}^{2},\quad E(\hat{S}_{cd})=S_{cd}.

An important practical question is to make inference about
\bar{c}
based on the simple random sample. This requires a more precise characterization of the distribution of its unbiased estimator
\hat{\bar{c}}
. The finite-sample exact distribution of
\hat{\bar{c}}
depends on the whole finite population
\{c_{1},\ldots,c_{n}\}
, which is unknown. The following finite population CLT characterizes the asymptotic distribution of
\hat{\bar{c}}
based on its first two moments.

Lemma 32.4
(finite population CLT)

Under Under simple random sampling, as
n\rightarrow\infty
, if

\frac{\max_{1\leq i\leq n}(c_{i}-\bar{c})^{2}}{\min(n_{1},n_{0})S_{c}^{2}}\rightarrow 0,

then

\frac{\hat{\bar{c}}-\bar{c}}{\sqrt{\frac{n_{0}}{nn_{1}}S_{c}^{2}}}\rightarrow\textsc{N}(0,1)

in distribution, and

\hat{S}_{c}^{2}/S_{c}^{2}\rightarrow 1

in probability.

Lemma
32.4
justifies the Wald-type
1-\alpha
confidence interval for
\bar{c}
:

\hat{\bar{c}}\pm z_{1-\alpha/2}\sqrt{\frac{n_{0}}{nn_{1}}\hat{S}_{c}^{2}}

where
z_{1-\alpha/2}
is the
1-\alpha/2
upper quantile of the standard Normal random variable.

Appendix 32.B
Proofs

Proof of Lemma
32.1
:

By symmetry, the
Z_{i}
’s have the same mean, so

n_{1}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}=E\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}\right)=nE(Z_{i})\Longrightarrow E(Z_{i})=n_{1}/n.

Because
Z_{i}
is a Bernoulli random variable, its variance is

\textup{var}(Z_{i})=\frac{n_{1}}{n}\left(1-\frac{n_{1}}{n}\right)=\frac{n_{1}n_{0}}{n^{2}}.

By symmetry again, the
Z_{i}
’s have the same variance and the pairs
(Z_{i},Z_{j})
’s have the same covariance, so

0=\textup{var}\left(\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}\right)=n\textup{var}(Z_{i})+n(n-1)\textup{cov}(Z_{i},Z_{j})

which implies that

\textup{cov}(Z_{i},Z_{j})=-\frac{n_{1}n_{0}}{n^{2}(n-1)}\quad(i\neq j).

\square

Proof of Lemma
32.2
:

The unbiasedness of the sample mean follows from linearity. For example,

E(\hat{\bar{c}})=E\left(\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}c_{i}\right)=\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}E(Z_{i})c_{i}=\bar{c}.

The covariance of the sample means is

\displaystyle\textup{cov}(\hat{\bar{c}},\hat{\bar{d}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\textup{cov}\left\{\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}(c_{i}-\bar{c}),\frac{1}{n_{1}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}(d_{i}-\bar{d})\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{1}^{2}}\left[\sum_{i=1}^{n}\textup{var}(Z_{i})(c_{i}-\bar{c})(d_{i}-\bar{d})+\sum_{i\neq j}\textup{cov}(Z_{i},Z_{j})(c_{i}-\bar{c})(d_{j}-\bar{d})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{1}^{2}}\left[\frac{n_{1}n_{0}}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}-\bar{c})(d_{i}-\bar{d})-\frac{n_{1}n_{0}}{n^{2}(n-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}(c_{i}-\bar{c})(d_{j}-\bar{d})\right].

Because

0=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}-\bar{c})\sum_{i=1}^{n}(d_{i}-\bar{d})=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}-\bar{c})(d_{i}-\bar{d})+\sum_{i\neq j}(c_{i}-\bar{c})(d_{j}-\bar{d}),

the covariance of the sample means reduces to

\displaystyle\textup{cov}(\hat{\bar{c}},\hat{\bar{d}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{1}{n_{1}^{2}}\left[\frac{n_{1}n_{0}}{n^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}-\bar{c})(d_{i}-\bar{d})+\frac{n_{1}n_{0}}{n^{2}(n-1)}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}-\bar{c})(d_{i}-\bar{d})\right]

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n_{0}}{nn_{1}}S_{cd}.

The variance formulas are just special cases with
\hat{\bar{c}}=\hat{\bar{d}}
.

\square

Proof of Lemma
32.3
:

We prove only the sample covariance term because the formulas for sample variances are special cases.
We have the following decomposition:

\displaystyle(n_{1}-1)\hat{S}_{cd}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}(c_{i}-\hat{\bar{c}})(d_{i}-\hat{\bar{d}})

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}\{(c_{i}-\bar{c})-(\hat{\bar{c}}-\bar{c})\}\{(d_{i}-\bar{d})-(\hat{\bar{d}}-\bar{d})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}(c_{i}-\bar{c})(d_{i}-\bar{d})-n_{1}(\hat{\bar{c}}-\bar{c})(\hat{\bar{d}}-\bar{d}).

Taking expectations on both sides, we have

\displaystyle E\{(n_{1}-1)\hat{S}_{cd}\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}E(Z_{i})(c_{i}-\bar{c})(d_{i}-\bar{d})-n_{1}E\{(\hat{\bar{c}}-\bar{c})(\hat{\bar{d}}-\bar{d})\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\frac{n_{1}}{n}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}-\bar{c})(d_{i}-\bar{d})-n_{1}\frac{n_{0}}{nn_{1}}S_{cd}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle S_{cd}\left\{\frac{n_{1}(n-1)}{n}-\frac{n_{0}}{n}\right\}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle(n_{1}-1)S_{cd},

and the conclusion follows by dividing both sides by
n_{1}-1
.

\square

Proof of Lemma
32.4
:

Hájek, 1960
gave a proof of the CLT for simple random sampling, and
Lehmann, 1975
gave a more accessible version of the proof.
Li and Ding, 2017
modified the CLT as presented in Lemma
32.4
, and proved the consistency of the sample variance. Due to the technical complexities, I omit the proof.

\square

Appendix 32.C
Comments on the literature

Survey sampling and experimental design have been deeply connected ever since
Neyman, 1934
;
Neyman, 1935
’s seminal work.
Li and Ding, 2017
and
Mukerjee et al., 2018
made many theoretical ties between these two areas.

Appendix 32.D
Homework Problems

AF.1
Sampling without replacement and the Hypergeometric distribution

Consider a special case of Lemma
32.2
with
c_{i}
’s being binary. Assume the total number of 1’s equals
T
so the total number of 0’s equals
n-T
. Let
t=\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}c_{i}
denote the total number of 1’s in the sample of size
n_{1}
.

Find the distribution, mean, and variance of
t
.

Remark:
t
follows a Hypergeometric distribution.

AF.2
Vector form of Lemma
32.2

Assume the
c_{i}
’s are vectors and define

S_{c}^{2}=(n-1)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}(c_{i}-\bar{c})(c_{i}-\bar{c})^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}},\quad\hat{S}_{c}^{2}=(n_{1}-1)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{n}Z_{i}(c_{i}-\hat{\bar{c}})(c_{i}-\hat{\bar{c}})^{\mkern-1.5mu\mathsf{T}}.

Show that

E(\hat{\bar{c}})=\bar{c},\quad\textup{cov}(\hat{\bar{c}})=\frac{n_{0}}{nn_{1}}S_{c}^{2},\quad E(\hat{S}_{c}^{2})=S_{c}^{2}.

Index
